>
> @annavsmith1   
> I think you may be talking past the professor rather than engaging with what he’s actually doing in the lecture.  
>   
> He isn’t denying that Macedonians were Greek-speaking or culturally Greek, nor that the Ptolemies were Macedonian Greeks, nor that Byzantium spoke Greek. All of that is well established.  
>   
> The point he’s making is about how identity functioned in antiquity. “Greek” was a broad cultural category, while Athenians, Spartans, Thebans, Macedonians, etc. were primary political and social identities. Those distinctions mattered historically, especially in Classical Greece, where many southern Greeks themselves debated Macedonian inclusion.  
>   
> Saying identities were plural and contextual is not the same as saying they were unrelated or “not Greek.” It’s a rejection of projecting modern nation-state ideas backward, not a denial of cultural continuity.  
>   
> Also, Greek etymology (e.g., Mesopotamia) or later Greek-speaking empires doesn’t collapse earlier ethnic, linguistic, and political diversity. Different periods, different questions.  
>   
> I’d say the lecture is about historical categories and not modern national legitimacy.   
>   
> Just my 2 cents

Thank you Professor Jiang @PredictiveHistory. I have one question that hopefully won't be lost in the flood of low effort comments.

In your last you video, you claimed that it is inevitable that Israel and its benefactor, the United States, will be heading towards a messy divorce at some point in the future, for reasons of sovereignty and rebellion against the imperial "divide and conquer" strategy. But is it even feasible for Israel to initiate such a conflict, given its relatively smaller stature?

To me, it seems much more feasible that Israel will simply find another benefactor. Let's look at history. The ancient Jews were relatively peaceful under the Achaemenids. Then, the Achaemenids were supplanted by the Greeks, and Judea naturally fell under Greek control. It was not until the Maccabee revolt (when the successor Greek states finally became too weak to exert control) that the independent Hasmonean Dynasty emerged and the Jewish people were able to have complete sovereignty (at least for a brief period of time until the Romans came). 

Granted, Israel has more agency and independence now under America's wing than the ancient Jews did as a vassal of King Cyrus... but the main problem is that Israel has far more ambitious goals that will engender conflict with its neighbors than its small size and power base could ever enable it to fulfill. It doesn't seem likely that Israel could "rebuff" America, at least not without finding a new benefactor.

4:12 It's very interesting how almost perfectly this description of the Chinese Warring States period also applies to the political situation in Mesoamerica when the Spanish arrived in 1519. War was very regulated and mostly avoided by doing human sacrifice, and the region was in a state of almost equilibrium in which the Triple Alliance extracted tributes from the other city states and these city states benefited through trade and by legitimizing their positions in power. When the Spanish arrived, most natives assumed they only wanted to trade and the Mexica tried to bribe them so they would leave, but they didn't. The head of the Triple Alliance, Motecuhzoma II, couldn't imagine why they would want to topple their political stability and take over the region (he may have been a bit superstitious and naive also).

Timestamps (Powered by Merlin AI)
00:05 - Geopolitical conflicts stem from elite competition and lack of loyalty.
03:00 - War often serves to maintain the status quo in history.
07:52 - Stability leads to decline and vulnerability in societies.
10:01 - The cycle of power shifts through temple wealth and mercenary conflicts.
14:43 - The Hellenistic World was shaped by key figures and systems like Homer and the polis.
17:00 - Sparta's conservatism contrasts sharply with Athens' expansionist democracy.
21:25 - Macedonian army, led by Alexander, conquered Persia through elite betrayal and tired leadership.
23:29 - The Hellenistic Age emerges after Alexander's conquests and leads to cultural syncretism.
27:45 - Aristotle's philosophy emphasizes material reality and purpose over the spirit world.
29:46 - The geography of Greece fosters innovation through conflict and access to trade.
33:52 - The Hellenistic military innovations stemmed from geographical limitations and cultural practices.
35:50 - The Battle of Marathon marked Greece's first major victory over Persia.
39:45 - The Battle of Salamis significantly shifted the power dynamics in the Hellenistic world.
41:42 - Athens' naval strength secures victory against Persia.
45:32 - Contrast between Greek values of liberty and Persian imperialism.
47:25 - Greeks' virtue shifts with the rise of the Athenian Empire.
51:28 - Sparta is reluctantly drawn into war by Athenian expansionism.
53:21 - Sparta avoids destroying Athens to maintain power balance.
57:19 - Pericles' strategies during the Athenian plague led to severe consequences.
59:21 - Athens transforms from liberator to oppressive empire after defeating Persia.
1:03:19 - Philip II established a meritocratic army and innovative tactics for conquest.
1:04:53 - Philip II unifies Greece before planning to conquer wealthy Persia.
1:08:50 - Alexander the Great believed he was divine, shaping his conquests and actions.
1:10:51 - Alexander's successors divided his empire into four regions after his death.
1:14:50 - The Library of Alexandria as a tool for cultural imperialism.
1:16:44 - Alexandria fostered Jewish scholarship, contributing to religious syncretism leading to Christianity.

### **@Steak514** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=UgxUabjnJQVa2HWhqPV4AaABAg)

I've always noticed the concept of equilibrium but never verbalized it.  Greece being the most clear example because every time a city state gained any power, they'd be countered by 2 other powerful actors in the region.  Sparta attacks Persia, Persia pays Athens or whoever a bunch of gold, Sparta has to retreat from Persia to fight off whoever Persia bribed.  And this happened again and again.  Very interesting concept. Breaking equilibrium creates new empires.  I remember thinking it was ironic that Macedonia conquered Greece after all the epic wars between Athens, Sparta, Thebes, Persia.  For an outsider to stroll in.  But like you say, that's actually the common outcome.  You could almost look at Rome's conquest of Europe like that.  Gaulic tribes warring with each other and Germany.  Basic equilibrium established for the most part.  They war back and forth but it's balanced.  Then a couple tribes ask Caesar for help, equilibrium is thrown out the window.  In this case Rome was fairly strong and not exactly an underdog, but it was still a major accomplishment to take France, Switzerland.

### **@platinumlifestylementors7408**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=UgzETb_EIIu9mrqLeNd4AaABAg)

@ProfessorJiang, I’m truly enjoying your lectures on secret and forgotten histories. Your work is brilliant, and you explain these patterns with rare clarity. I love the link to who we are!



But I must add something from an African—especially a Sub-Saharan African—perspective: none of this is new to us. Our ancestors preserved strange whispers of this same secret history through oral traditions long before it was written anywhere.



What’s truly shocking is how the deeper you dig, the more you find that the histories of the Levant, Israel, Europe, South America, and even China mirror older African narratives. The rivers, locations, artifacts, cosmologies(especially of Europe is predominantly a recast and recreation of what took place in the Southern of Africa)—many of them were pointed out to us by elders who never stepped into a modern classroom, yet their descriptions match what archaeology is only now beginning to uncover.  Most of these same prominent sites still live in Africa today.



If one goes beyond the colonial versions of “African history” in European books and into the real ancient record—oral, symbolic, astronomical—you will find disturbances on every front: education, spirituality, metaphysics, and even technologies the modern world has not yet caught up to.



Even the Bible, if taken seriously, points the same direction. The movements of the patriarchs, the migrations to and from Egypt (Kemet)-were up and down the Nile, the descriptions of the people, the soil, the minerals, the languages, the rituals—all of these still exist across Sub-Saharan Africa today. The land still literally flows with milk and honey: cattle, goats, wild honey, rich forests, fresh water, beautiful mountains, animals and wild beast, natural golf courses and fertile valleys.



And when you trace it back far enough—past Kemet, past Sumer—you arrive at places like Adam’s Calendar in Southern Africa, where the story truly begins.



Your work is uncovering something profound. The time you will decide to touch Africa be ready to be disturbed ; it has been holding the keys to this “hidden history” the entire time. Keep going—you will see greater secrets.

### **@adrianaslund8605**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=UgysT7FJi7KFNuXxwyl4AaABAg)

The way he talked about china does bring to mind the hoplite phalanx way of fighting. In which the one furthest to the right was relatively exposed and sometimes when they were unwilling to advance the phalanx would turn right while the other phalanx also turned right having them walk past eachother. Point is. It was very much a formulaic and ritualized way of combat. Which made them quite weak to someone like Philip of Macedon who used combined arms(with swordmen protecting the flank of the phalanx and plenty of cavalry) and flanking tactics. The tercio in europe was kind of like a later equivalent that the swedish king broke in a very similar way by instead of having soldiers fire at long range for fear of getting to close they were expected to march up to close proximity before firing and very mobile horse artillery to break their formations up and light horsemen to cut them up while they are dazed from the salvo.

### **@MrRadamantz**

They were alliances against Qin, but they failed.

318 BCE Battle of Hangu Pass: A coalition of five states (led by Wei and Zhao) invaded Qin but was repelled at the strategic Hangu Pass, a natural defensive chokepoint. Qin then counterattacked, defeating the allies and executing their commander.

Early 3rd century BCE: Multiple coalitions formed, but they fractured quickly. For instance, in 293 BCE, Qin crushed a joint Han–Wei force, killing over 120,000 soldiers. Alliances often collapsed due to Qin's diplomacy—offering land or titles to one state to betray the others.

### **@RyanTrinh33**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=UgzpLakMFJQIo2LLWl94AaABAg)

I can understand why people say that the elites of an empire will gradually become lazy, stupid, and arrogant, and will eventually be overthrown by the peasants within the empire and by foreign powers on its borders (who are seeking opportunities to elevate their own social status). This pattern repeatedly happened to the royal families of Chinese feudal dynasties.
Let’s take the Ming Dynasty as an example: the Hongwu Emperor and the Yongle Emperor were both highly successful in founding and consolidating the state. Several generations after them also did reasonably well in maintaining stability. But as time went on, the imperial family became increasingly corrupt and degenerate, eventually leading to the dynasty being invaded and overthrown by an outside power - the Qing.

This pattern can somehow be applied to changing our own fate: for example, someone who comes from a weaker branch of a large clan in which other branches are wealthier or more influential - over several generations, the same cycle may repeat, and the balance of power may eventually shift according to this very rule.

I can’t help myself but ask: have you ever read The Hero with a Thousand Faces by Joseph Campbell?
The book explores the “Hero’s Journey,” a narrative pattern that appears in myths from cultures all over the world. Campbell connects these shared story structures to Carl Jung’s ideas about archetypes and the collective unconscious—suggesting that these mythic patterns reflect something deep and universal in the human psyche.

### **@halcyon-369**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=UgwMOHJGmPrhWvcM8xV4AaABAg)

In Sparta specifically:

An older male citizen (erastes) would choose a promising adolescent boy (usually mid-teens).

The relationship was social, educational, and moral, intended to model ideal Spartan virtues: courage, discipline, loyalty, and military excellence.

It was meant to help integrate the youth into the adult warrior society.

Was it sexual?

This is where interpretations differ.

Ancient authors who write about Sparta (Plutarch, Xenophon, Aristotle) disagree:

Xenophon and Plutarch claim the relationship was non-sexual, focusing on moral mentorship and character shaping.

Aristotle and some later sources suggest that sexual or romantic elements may have existed, but they also emphasize that Spartan law viewed such acts as dishonorable or corrupt.

Modern historians generally conclude:

👉 The Spartan system centered on mentorship.
👉 Sexual activity may have occurred in some cases, but it was not the institutional norm and likely discouraged.

Aristotle and Alexander the Great were not childhood friends; Aristotle was Alexander’s tutor when Alexander was about 13–16 years old, so their relationship was primarily that of teacher and pupil rather than playmates of the same generation.�� Aristotle was in his early 40s when Philip II of Macedon invited him to educate the young prince at Mieza, which makes a childhood friendship between them chronologically impossible.� Ancient sources instead emphasize Alexander’s statement that he owed his father his life but owed Aristotle “the dignity of life,” highlighting respect and intellectual influence rather than a shared childhood

### **@grafiksanatci**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=z0AwFin83lo&lc=Ugw3R7Jq_uf8JKdhK7F4AaABAg)

The claim that wars are fought to preserve the status quo



In the video, it is argued that most wars occur in order to keep society occupied or to reduce competition among elites. This view reflects a somewhat **geopolitical determinist** perspective. In reality, however, the causes of war are usually **multi-layered** and complex.



Common causes of war include:



* security concerns

* economic interests

* ideology

* leadership mistakes or miscalculations

* alliance systems



For example, **World War I** cannot be explained solely by elite competition. Rather, it was the result of multiple interacting factors.



It was not only elite rivalry but also:



* nationalism

* the arms race

* the alliance system

* the Balkan crises



All of these factors together contributed to the outbreak of the war.
